Both `/app/answer.py` and `/app/answer.json` exist, and the JSON contains the propagation order.\n\n**Final order:** CORE, PRIMARY_LOOP, STEAM_GEN, COOLING, TURBINE.\n\nEstimated physical onset of each subsystem, all in UTC on 2024-01-16, with 95% bootstrap intervals:\n\n| Subsystem | Onset | Interval |\n|---|---|---|\n| CORE | 00:01 | 23:54 to 00:06 |\n| PRIMARY_LOOP | 00:18 | 00:13 to 00:22 |\n| STEAM_GEN | 00:47 | 00:45 to 00:49 |\n| COOLING | 01:05 | 01:03 to 01:07 |\n| TURBINE | 01:20 | 01:19 to 01:21 |\n\n**How I got there**\n\n- **Time alignment.** The three loggers run on different clock offsets. I converted every reading to UTC using the offset column in the metadata, then shifted each sensor back by its response delay.\n- **Baseline model.** Many temperature sensors carry a clear 24-hour diurnal cycle. I fit a sinusoidal baseline on the first day and worked with residuals so the cycle was not mistaken for drift.\n- **Unrelated anomalies removed.** Three sensors flatlined at exact constants for 20 to 25 minutes on the evening before the event: turbine output, steam pressure, and cooling pump pressure. Zero noise and round values mark these as stuck-sensor faults, not physical events, so I masked them. They explain the earliest alarms.\n- **Onset estimation.** For each subsystem I fit a shared change point across its four sensors, each with its own slope and delay. Three variants of the analysis, with and without diurnal correction and with different fit windows, all produced the same order. A block bootstrap gave probability 1.0 for every adjacent pair in the ranking.\n\n**Why the alarm order is misleading.** Alarms fired in the reverse order: turbine first, then cooling, steam, primary loop, and core last. That is because the originating core fault ramps very slowly, while downstream subsystems respond faster and cross alarm thresholds sooner.
